Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
Note
The Annual Report 2019 was examined and adopted by the
Supervisory Board of the National Bank of Moldova at meeting of
May 21, 2020 and was submitted to the Parliament of the
Republic of Moldova in accordance with the provisions of Law no.
548/1995 on the National Bank of Moldova (republished in
Monitorul Oﬁcial of the Republic of Moldova, 2015, no. 297-300,
art. 544, with subsequent amendments).
The report was compiled using the latest statistical data held by
the National Bank of Moldova, the National Bureau of Statistics,
the Ministry of Economy and Infrastructure and the Ministry of
Finance.
Also, were used data provided by international organizations and
central banks of neighboring countries.
Computation of some statisitcal data was conducted by the
National Bank of Moldova.
All rights reserved. No part of this publication may be reproduced,
and the use of data in studies is allowed with the proper
speciﬁcation of the source.

3
Foreword
2019 was marked by many events, both externally and internally,
all with an impact on economic activity, prices and national
currency.
Externally, 2019 was dominated by trade disputes
between the US and China, preparations for Brexit and the
slowdown in economic growth in several developed countries of
the world, internally we had political instability and the change of
two governments.
Although more modest than in previous years, the annual dynamics
of economic activity was positive in 2019, the gross domestic
product being by 3.6% higher than the previous year. The increase
in disposable income of the population was less reﬂected in private
consumption, but had a more pronounced impact on investment.
Most sectors of the national economy registered positive dynamics.
However, construction and internal trade continued to play a
dominant role in economic growth. At the same time, adverse
weather conditions for certain crops had a negative impact on the
performance of the agricultural sector in 2019.
During 2019, the annual inﬂation rate outlined a sharp upward
trajectory, supported, on the one hand, by a positive domestic
demand and, on the other hand, by the incidence of distinct
phenomena
such
as:
ﬁscal
policy
adjustments,
currency
depreciation adverse weather conditions, as well as electricity
tariff increases.
Despite these factors, we managed to keep the average annual
inﬂation, which was 4.8%, very close to the inﬂation target.
Although at the end of the year, inﬂation increased over the
variation corridor targeted by the National Bank, we forecast in
2020 a rapid decrease of thereof.
The monetary policy measures adopted by the National Bank
aimed at tempering the forecasted variations of the consumer


4
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
price index. In June and July, monetary policy rates were raised
in order to moderate price increases.
At the end of the year,
according to the forecast of rapid decrease of the price level, the
interest rates and the size of the banks’ required reserves for lei
holdings were decreased. The National Bank will further relax
monetary conditions and provide liquidity to the banking sector,
depending on price developments and the needs of the economy.
The banking system has undergone a major transformation
process in recent years, which we can consider acheived. The
transformations focused on the aspects of banking activity – the
legislative and normative framework, shareholding and corporate
administration, client relationships and products offered.
The
Basel III principles have been implemented, which include a
comprehensive set of provisions on the size of banks’ capital and
capital buffers, risk assessment and prevention, internal and
external control of banks’ operations. During the year, there were
several large transactions with the shares of some of our banks,
the market continuing to enter reputable foreign investors, with
access to international ﬁnancial markets, ensuring adequate
governance. After the transformation period, the banking system
resumed its full operation: and, respectively, the volume of the
loan portfolio began to grow, banks offered new credit products,
including in the ﬁeld of mortgages and consumer loans, the
economy began to assimilate the excessive liquidity accumulated
in recent years.
The National Bank has maintained its institutional and decision-
making independence, cooperating with state institutions and
decision-makers in the areas of its statutory activities.
The diligent management of the state’s foreign exchange reserves,
the ﬁnancing from external partners and the foreign exchange
intervention policy promoted by the National Bank increased the
value of the ofﬁcial reserve assets to over USD 3 billion. Foreign
exchange reserves provide for the possibility of moderating the
excessive volatility of the exchange rate, are the guarantee of
meeting the external obligations of the state and, in the event of
a signiﬁcant slowdown in exports, would be necessary resources
to support several months of imports.
Given the situation at the end of 2019, we can say that our banking
system is in a pretty good condition, both organizationally and
ﬁnancially. Currently, given the epidemiological situation and the
problems caused by the slowdown in economic activities, the
health of the banking system is more important than ever. The
high level of liquidity, the large volume of ofﬁcial reserve assets,
the robust capitalization of banks will allow us to overcome the
created situation, maintaining the conﬁdence of our citizens in
the national currency and in the ﬁnancial-banking system of the
Republic of Moldova.
Octavian Armas, u
Governor


5
Summary
International context
The
year
2019
was
highlighted
by
trade
tensions,
Brexit
negotiations and the decline of industrial production in Germany.
During 2019, the USA and China imposed several trade tariffs on
each other, the negotiation rounds often ending without success.
The exit of the United Kingdom from the European Union
scheduled for 29 March, 2019 was postponed twice, on 31
October, 2019 and 31 January, 2020. The German economy faced
a signiﬁcant contraction in industrial production, after on 1
January, 2019 new carbon emission standards that have affected
the car industry have come into force. If in the ﬁrst half of 2019
the prospects for economic growth in many economies were
favorable, in the second half it was more and more about a global
economic moderation. In the second semester 2019, the Federal
Reserve System has decreased the range of interest rates
threefold amid the slowdown in global demand. Brent’s quotation
of oil fell overall in 2019, with the oil market swinging between
meeting the OPEC+ agreement to limit oil production and
moderating global demand. There was a slight economic boom in
the region, without signiﬁcant shocks.
Economic developments
In 2019, the GDP recorded an increase of 3.6%, being lower
than in 2018. The evolution of economic activity in 2019 was
largely determined by domestic demand, both for consumption
and investment. In this respect, household consumption was 3.0%
higher than the previous year, and the gross ﬁxed capital increased
by 12.9%. Both the evolution of private consumption and that of
investments were mainly supported by the increase in disposable
income of the population during 2019. At the same time, economic
growth is due to the 7.3% increase in exports compared to 2018.
Final consumption of public administration contracted by 0.5%
compared to the previous year. Imports in 2019 increased by
6.7% compared to 2018. In the sectors of the national economy,
the gross added value in construction was 15.9% higher than
in 2018. The commercial sector recorded an increase of 5.7%.
Positive contributions to GDP dynamics were also generated by
the increase in gross added value in industry and in information
and communication sector.
The evolution of the ﬁnancial and
insurance sector, as well as that of real estate transactions, has
had a positive but modest impact on GDP growth. At the same
time, net product taxes increased by 1.4% as compared to 2018.
A negative impact on economic growth in 2019 was exercised by
the dynamics of the agricultural sector.


6
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
Inﬂation
In 2019, the annual inﬂation rate was 4.8%, being higher than in
2018. During 2019, the annual inﬂation rate had a pronounced
upward trajectory. It increased from 0.9% in December 2018 to
7.5% in December 2019. The upward trajectory of the annual
inﬂation rate was supported, according to estimates, by a positive
aggregate demand. At the same time, at the beginning of 2019
the upward trajectory of the annual inﬂation rate was determined
by some ﬁscal policy adjustments that accelerated core inﬂation,
as well as the effect of a short base period in the ﬁrst quarter of
2018 for regulated prices. Subsequently, this trend was supported
by the depreciation of the national currency, as well as the effects
of adverse weather conditions for some crops, which exerted
inﬂationary pressures on food prices and a growing contribution by
the end of the year from this subcomponent. In the third quarter of
2019, regulated prices generated additional pressures as a result
of the adjustment of the electricity tariff. At the same time, towards
the end of 2019, the above-mentioned inﬂationary pressures were
partially mitigated by the atypical hot weather which delayed and
diminished the impact of the positive seasonal effect characteristic
of that period on food and fuel prices.
Monetary policy
During 2019, the NBM increased the base rate in June and July by
0.5 percentage points, to 7.5%, after which, at the last monetary
policy meeting in 2019, the Executive Board of the NBM decided
to reduce the base rate by 2.0 percentage points to 5.5%.
The NBM decreased the norm of minimum required reserves from
funds attracted in Moldovan lei from 42.5% to 41.0%, the
maintenance period being in the ﬁrst quarter of 2020. The norm
of minimum required reserves from funds attracted in freely
convertible currency was increased in July 2019 by 3.0 percentage
points, up to the level of 17.0% and in December 2019 by 3.0
percentage points, up to the level of 20.0%, the last decision
being applied in the ﬁrst quarter of 2020.
The annual growth rate of the broad money supply in 2019 was
8.2%. M2 Monetary aggregate (money supply M2 includes money
in circulation (M0), deposits in lei and money market instruments)
increased by 8.2% annually, money in circulation increased by
8.9% annually, monetary base broadly increased by 7.6% annually.
The average interest rate on new loans granted in the national
currency in 2019 was 8.24% annually, at the same time, the
average rate on new deposits attracted was 4.63% annually.
Banking system
At the end of 2019, the banking sector of the Republic of Moldova
included 11 banks licensed by the National Bank of Moldova (NBM).


Summary
7
During 2019, the National Bank continued its efforts to ensure
sound corporate governance,
transparency of shareholders,
attracting potential investors and reducing the risks to which
banks are exposed, in order to maintain a stable banking sector.
Thus, during the year, some modiﬁcations occurred in the
shareholding structure of three systemic banks:
• As of 22 January 2019, the Executive Board of the NBM
granted prior approval for the acquisition by "Doverie —
Invest" S.A. (Bulgaria) of the qualiﬁed holding in the share
capital of BC "Moldindconbank" S.A. in amount of 63.89%. In
February 2019, the said package of shares was purchased
by the Public Property Agency.
Subsequently, during the
auction, the package of shares in the amount of 63.89%
of the share capital of BC "Moldindconbank" S.A. was sold
to "Doverie — Invest" S.A. During 2019, the shareholder
"Doverie — Invest" S.A. increased its qualiﬁed holding up
to 77.63%, as a result of the completion of the offer to take
over the bank’s shares (13.73%) and the acquisition of the
holding from others shareholders of the bank (0.01%).
• As of 28 February 2019, the Executive Board of the NBM
granted prior approval for the acquisition of qualiﬁed holding
in amount of 48.37% of the share capital of BC "MOLDOVA-
AGROINDBANK" S.A. by the group of persons consisting of
"HEIM PARTNERS LIMITED" and 5 other shareholders of the
bank under the usufruct Contract concluded.
Thus, the
consortium of investors formed by EBRD, Invalda INVL and
Horizon Capital through "HEIM PARTNERS LIMITED" controls
48.37% of the share capital of BC "MOLDOVA-AGROINDBANK"
S.A.
• As of 10 July 2019, the Executive Board of the NBM approved
the acquisition by OTP Bank Nyrt., Hungary, of the share
control package (more than 50,0%) of the share capital of
Mobiasbanca - OTP Group S.A. Thus, in the context of the
registration of the ownership right of new shareholder OTP
BANK Nyrt., the ofﬁcial name of the bank was changed to
Mobiasbanca - OTP Group S.A., registered the Statute of
the Bank in new wording, the new composition of the Bank
Council, and the Chairman of the Board - CEO.
In 2019, in the banking sector the trend of consolidation of own
funds continued, which amounted to MDL 11.5 billion, increasing
by 5.6%.
Banks complied with the requirement for the Total capital ratio
indicator, including capital buffers. The rate of total own funds
on the banking sector, according to the data presented by the
licensed banks, registered the value of 25.3%, decreasing by 1.2
percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
At the same time,
banks maintained a high liquidity and
proﬁtability level.
Therefore, the value of the long-term liquidity indicator (principle I
of liquidity) amounted to 0.7 (limit ≤1), at the same level as at the
end of 2018.


8
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
The current liquidity of the banking sector (principle II of liquidity)
diminished by 4.0 percentage points, to 50.6% (limit ≥20%),
remaining at one of the highest levels in the region, more than half
of the assets of the banking sector being concentrated in liquid
assets.
As of 2019, the proﬁt of the banking sector accounted for MDL
2.3 billion, increasing compared to the previous year by 55.5%.
For 2019, the rentability of assets1 and rentability of capital2 of
licensed banks recorded values of 2.5% and 14.6%, respectively,
increasing by 0.8 percentage points and by 4.3 percentage points
respectively, compared to the end of the previous year.
In addition, the amounts of assets and deposits increased by 9.1%
to MDL 90.6 billion and by 7.7% to MDL 68.4 billion. Compared to
the end of the previous year, the bank’s loan portfolio increased
by 13.9% to MDL 40.4 billion. During the year, the share of non-
performing loans in loan portfolios decreased and constitued 8.5%.
1Rentability of assets=annualized net income/the average of assets
2Rentability of capital=annualized net income/the average of capital

signiﬁcant contraction in industrial production, after on 1 January
2019 new carbon emission standards that have affected the car
industry have come into force.
If in the ﬁrst half of 2019 the
prospects
for
economic
growth
in
many
economies
were
favorable, in the second half it was more and more about a global
economic moderation. In the second half of 2019, the Federal
Reserve System has decreased the range of interest rates
threefold amid the slowdown in global demand. Brent’s quotation
of oil fell overall in 2019, with the oil market swinging between
meeting the OPEC+ agreement to limit oil production and
moderate global demand. There was a slight economic boom in
the region, without signiﬁcant shocks.
In January 2020, the IMF3 reported that the world economy grew in
2019 by an average of 2.9% compared to 3.6% in 2018. Advanced
economies have recorded an average increase of 1.7%, and the
emerging and developing ones of 3.7%, in both cases the increases
being signiﬁcantly lower compared to 2018.
The US economy grew in 2019 by 2.3% compared to 2.9% in
2018, due to the effects of the second round of trade tariffs
imposed on other economies. Japan’s economy is one of the few
advanced economies that in 2019 registered a higher economic
growth compared to 2018 (0.7% vs. 0.3%). The UK economy
grew by 1.4%, about as much as in 2018, as a result of the
continuation of Brexit negotiations for a longer period than initially
assumed. In the euro area in 2019 there was a sharper moderation
of the economy, the cumulative gross domestic product growing
by 1.2% compared to 1.9% in 2018. The German economy faced
a signiﬁcant contraction in industrial production after on 1 January,
2019, new carbon emission standards that affected the automotive
industry came into force. Overall, Germany’s economy grew by
only 0.6% in 2019 compared to 1.5% in 2018. At the same time,
other large economies in the euro area recorded the following
increases: France – 1.3%, Spain – 2.0% and Italy – 0.3%. China’s
3IMF, World Economic Outlook Update – January 2020

economy grew by 6.1% in 2019 compared to 6.7% in 2018 (Chart

In the region, neighboring economies have progressed to a lesser
extent than in the previous year. Thus, in 2019 the Romanian
economy grew by 4.1%, the economy of the Russian Federation
by 1.3%, the economy of Ukraine by 3.2%.
For comparison,
the economy of the Republic of Moldova grew in 2019 by 3.6%
(Subchapter 1.3 will describe in more detail the developments of
neighboring economies and trading partners) (Chart 1.1).

South African rand
After the Federal Reserve System gradually increased the interest
rate range to 2.25-2.5% in 2018, starting with the second half of
2019, it relaxed monetary policy, reducing the interest rate range
by three times to 0.25 percentage points. The monetary easing by
the FRS was a major factor in the downward pressure on the US
dollar. At the same time, amid worsening trade tensions between
the US and China, the US dollar appreciated signiﬁcantly. The
USDX index recorded a 3.8% growth in 2019, which is an average
appreciation of the US dollar against the other 6 major currencies4
(Chart 1.2).
Compared to previous years, in 2019 there were no turbulences
on the foreign exchange market. Moreover, by the end of the year,
the volatility of the EUR/USD pair had reached a very low level.
On average, the single European currency appreciated in 2019
by 5.2% against the US dollar, but the value at the end of the
year was 2.4% more depreciated than at the end of 2018. The
pound sterling depreciated in 2019 on average by 4.5% against
the US dollar, but towards the end of the year, when the Brexit
agreement was approved by the British Parliament, the pound
sterling managed to recover, appreciating by 3.3% against the US
dollar (Table 1.1).
In 2019, against the background of the continuous appreciation
of the US dollar and the increase of uncertainties related to trade
tensions, most currencies of emerging and developing economies
depreciated against the US dollar. The signiﬁcant depreciation
of the Argentine peso and the Venezuelan bolivar reﬂects the
precarious situation in those economies. The Turkish lira continued
to depreciate due to the consequences of US sanctions in 2018.
The Russian ruble depreciated by an average of 3.5% against the
US dollar, but towards the end of the year, due to rising oil prices,
the Russian ruble succeeded to appreciate by 6.5% against the US
dollar. The Ukrainian hryvnia appreciated on the background of the
economic recovery by an average of 5.0% against the US dollar,
the value in December 2019 being 15.0% more appreciated than
the value in December 2018. In 2019, the Romanian leu, the Czech
koruna, The Polish zloty and the Hungarian forint depreciated
against the US dollar, largely reﬂecting the trend of the single
European currency against the US dollar, but also the domestic
problems facing those economies. For comparison, in 2019 the
Moldovan leu depreciated by an average of 4.6% against the US
dollar (Table 1.1).
4USDX Index is calculated by weighted geometric mean value of the US dollar
against a basket of foreign currencies: the euro – 57.6%, the Japanese yen – 13.6%,
the Pound sterling – 11.9%, the Canadian dollar – 9.1%, the Swedish crown – 4.2%,
the Swiss franc – 3.6%.


Chapter 1. External environment
11
International prices averaged a slight upward trend in 2019. Oil
prices were supported by the extension of the OPEC+ agreement,
which involves limiting oil production by 1.7 million barrels per day.
The concerted action of OPEC+ member countries has led to a
balanced oil market. However, fears about economic moderation
related to rising trade tensions led to an overall decrease in oil
prices by 8.7% (Urals) compared to 2018. Taking into account

of natural gas in the Republic of Moldova increased on average by
7.5%. An upward trend also occured in the case of international
food prices (FAO index), which increased by an average of 1.8%
amid limited supply in the face of increased demand, but also
the disruption of the logistics chain of international trade in food
products. Prices for metals increased in 2019 by an average of
6.8%, of which for precious metals by 10.6% and for base metals
by 3.7%. Precious metals prices rose sharply in 2019 in response
to increased global uncertainty and accommodative monetary
policies (Chart 1.3).
1.2
Evolution of important economies
United States of America
In 2019, the the US gross domestic product increased by 2.3%,
slower by 0.6 percentage points as compared to 2018.
The
moderation of economic growth in the US, especially in the
second half of 2019, was caused by the negative effects of trade
tensions that affected international trade and quotations on raw
materials. The unemployment rate in the US fell from 4.0% in
January 2019 to 3.5% in December 2019. Another key indicator –
the average inﬂation rate – was 1.8% in 2019, well below the 2.4%
rate recorded in 2018 and below the 2.0% target level of theFRS.
The persistence of disinﬂationary pressures determined FRS to
decrease the range of interest rates three times, each time by
0.25 percentage points.
Euro area
In 2019, the euro area economy grew by 1.2%, the lowest increase
since 2014. Industrial production in the euro area contracted by
an average of 1.5%, after the automotive sector in Germany has
been affected by the new carbon emission requirements. At the
same time, construction production increased by an average of
1.9%, imports by 1.5%, and exports increased by only 2.7%, being
affected by the US introduction of tariffs on steel imports and
aluminum but also by the worsening climate of international trade.
The unemployment rate decreased in 2019, reaching the level
of 7.4% in December. As domestic demand continued to fall and
international prices declined or rose to a lesser extent, consumer
prices in the euro area rose by an average of only 1.2% (Table 1.2).
China
China’s economy grew by 6.1% in 2019, with 0.6 percentage points
less than in 2018, at the same time, with the lowest growth rate
since 1990. The economic downturn in 2019 was caused by trade
tensions between the US and China.

The economy of the Russian Federation grew in 2019 by an
average of 1.3%, with 1.2 percentage points less than in 2018. The
anemic growth of the Russian Federation’s economy was caused
by the persistence of low oil prices, and by international sanctions
imposed on them. During 2019, industrial production increased
by an average of 2.4%. For the ﬁrst time in many years, exports
from the Russian Federation contracted by an average of 6.0%,
reﬂecting the reduction in oil production, following the extension
of the OPEC+ agreement, but also the decline in international
trade as a whole. At the same time, imports into the Russian
Federation increased by an average of 2.2%, which indicates a
relatively low domestic demand. Towards the end of 2019, the
rise in oil prices accentuated the current account surplus, which
signiﬁcantly contributed to the appreciation of the Russian ruble.
In the second half of 2019, consumer prices fell below the level set
by the Central Bank of the Russian Federation of 4.0% (average
annual inﬂation in 2019 – 4.5%, annual inﬂation in December 2019
– 3.1%), which determined the monetary authority to decrease 5
times the base rate, from 7.75 to 6.25% (Table 1.2).
Ukraine
The gross domestic product in Ukraine increased by an average of
3.2% in 2019, slightly below the pace recorded in 2018. During the
analyzed period, industrial production contracted by an average of
0.5%, the agricultural production increased on average by 1.1%,
and the construction activity by 23.6%. At the same time, exports
and imports increased on average by 11.2 and 6.0%, respectively.
The easing of inﬂationary pressures during 2019 determined the
moderation of the annual inﬂation rate to 4.1% in December 2019,
the annual average being 7.9% (Table 1.2).


Chapter 1. External environment
13
Turkey
The Turkish economy gradually adapted after the currency shock
of August 2018, the gross domestic product increasing in 2019
by 0.9%. At the same time, the annual inﬂation rate moderated
to 11.8% in December 2019, but the average annual inﬂation
was 15.2%. The Central Bank of Turkey maintained a constant
monetary policy rate of 24.0% in the ﬁrst half of 2019, but the
outlook for inﬂation moderation in the second half of 2019 led to a
signiﬁcant reduction in interest rates, in total with 12.0 percentage
points.
Belarus
In 2019, the economy of the Republic of Belarus grew by 1.2%,
with 1.9 percentage points less than the previous year. At the
same time, consumer prices had a steadily declining evolution,
registering an annual inﬂation rate in December 2019 of 4.7%.

Gross domestic product
In 2019, GDP registered an increase of 3.6%, by 0.4 percentage
points lower than in 2018 (Chart 2.1).
During the year the
evolution of the economic activity recorded some ﬂuctuations. In
the ﬁrst part of the year, it showed an upward trend, registering
the value of 5.8% in the second quarter.
Subsequently, the
situation reversed and the annual growth rate of GDP moderated
to 0.2% by the end of 2019.
In the ﬁrst half of the year the
economy of the Republic of Moldova was supported mainly by a
signiﬁcant contribution from exports and increased investment
which determined a pronounced dynamics of the construction
sector. Despite the signiﬁcant increase in the disposable income
of the population, private consumption had a modest dynamic in
the ﬁrst half of the year.
However, it accelerated in the third
quarter of 2019, exerting a signiﬁcant impact on economic growth.
Towards the end of 2019, in the context of the moderation of the
growth of the disposable income of the population, of a less
favorable
external
environment,
as
well
as
of
a
modest
performance in the agricultural sector, the economic growth was
marginal (Charts 2.2 and 2.3).
From the perspective of uses, the evolution of economic activity in
2019 was largely determined by domestic demand, both for
consumption and for investment.
In this sense, household
consumption was 3.0% higher than the previous year, and gross
ﬁxed capital formation increased by 12.9%. These developments
determined a contribution of 5.6 percentage points to GDP
dynamics. Both the evolution of private consumption and that of
investments were supported, mainly, by the increase of the
disposable income of the population during 2019. However, the
stock change component had a negative impact of 0.6 percentage
points to GDP dynamics. At the same time, a signiﬁcant part (2.2
percentage points) of economic growth in 2019 can be attributed
to the increase in exports by 7.3% compared to 2018 which was
mainly due to the evolution of agriculture in 2018.
Final
consumption of public administration in 2019 contracted by 0.5%
compared to the previous year. The dynamics of economic activity
in 2019 was mitigated by the increase in imports by 6.7%
compared to 2018 which generated a negative impact of 3.7
percentage points, but was supported by the increase in domestic
demand.
By resource categories (Chart 2.4), the GDP growth in 2019 was
largely
determined
by
the
positive
contribution
from
the

Positive but smaller
contributions were generated by the increase in gross value
added in industry and in the information and communications
sector by 2.6% and 9.2%, respectively.

insurance sector, as well as that of real estate transactions
registered an increase of 4.4% and 1.2%, respectively. However,
their contribution to GDP dynamics was minor (0.1 percentage
points each). At the same time, the net taxes on products in 2019
increased by 1.4%, contributing by 0.2 percentage points to GDP
dynamics.
A negative impact of 0.2 percentage points was
exercised by contracting the agricultural sector6 by 2.3%.
Consumption of the population
In 2019, the ﬁnal consumption of population increased by 3.0%,
registering a lower rate by 0.8 percentage points than in 2018
(Chart 2.5). The respective dynamics of population consumption
was supported by the acceleration of the increase of expenditures
for the procurement of services up to 5.3%, but also of the
expenditures for the procurement of goods. Thus, they were 2.3%
higher than in 2018.
The increase in population consumption in 2019 was supported by
the increase of its main sources of ﬁnancing (salaries, pensions,
remittances, loans). Thus, in 2019, they, according to estimates,
increased in real terms by about 10.0% (Chart 2.6). This dynamic
was largely determined by the income from employment and
new loans granted to individuals.
At the same time, positive
contributions of a smaller magnitude were also exerted by the
dynamics of remittances and incomes related to social insurance.
Investments
Although investments had a signiﬁcant positive impact on GDP,
in 2019, their growth rate was lower than in the previous year.
In 2019, the annual rate of gross capital formation slowed down
compared to 2018, due to the moderation of investments in gross
ﬁxed capital formation and the negative impact of the change
in inventories (Chart 2.7). Thus, the annual rate of gross ﬁxed
capital formation in 2019 had a level of 12.9% or 1.6 percentage
points lower than in 2018. The change in inventories generated a
negative impact of 0.6 percentage points to GDP dynamics.
The main source of ﬁnancing investments in ﬁxed assets were
investments made from the own means of enterprises and the
population (Chart 2.8). At the same time, unlike in 2018, when
the investments from the funds of the national public budget
contributed essentially to the acceleration of the annual rate of
investments, in 2019 their contribution was clearly lower.

Agricultural production
In 2019,
global agricultural production decreased by 1.9%
compared to 2018 (Chart 2.9), due to the decrease in the volume
of animal production by 6.0%.
The contraction in production
volume in the livestock sector was determined by the negative
evolution of "milk" production (-10.9%), meat in live weight
(-5.0%) and eggs (-2.2%).

At the same time, in 2019 the volume of vegetable production was
0.3% lower than in 2018. Thus, the structure recorded a decrease
in the production of "grapes", "sugar beet", "fruits, nuts, berries"
and "wheat".
At the same time, the harvest of "vegetables",
"sunﬂower", "corn for grains" and "soybean" was higher than in
2018.
Industrial production
In 2019, the volume of industrial production on all forms of
ownership increased in real terms by 2.0% compared to 2018
(Chart 2.10).
This evolution was mainly determined by the
increase of production in the manufacturing industry (the
subcomponent with the largest share) by 3.1%. At the same time,
in the extractive industry sectors and in the "production and
distribution of electricity and heat, gas, hot water and air
conditioning" there were decreases of 1.9 and 4.0% of production
volume, respectively. In the manufacturing industry, the most
signiﬁcant increases were registered in the following industrial
activities:
"manufacturing
of
motor
vehicles,
trailers
and
semi-trailers" (24.4%), "metal construction industry and metal
products, excluding machinery, equipment and installations"
(18.8%), "manufacture of rubber and plastics products" (17.8%),
"metallurgical industry" (12.5%) and "furniture manufacturing"
(12.0%).
Freight shipping
In 2019, rail, road, river and air transport companies transported
3.7% less freight compared to 2018 (Chart 2.11). This decrease
was determined by the annual decrease in the volume of freight
transported by rail (13.6%) and road (0.4%). At the same time,
freight shipping by air and river increased in annual terms by 18.3
and 2.0%, respectively.

Retail trade
In 2019, the volume of turnover generated by retail sales
enterprises as their main activity increased by 13.2% compared to
2018, increasing by 5.6 percentage points compared to 2018. At
the same time, the turnover generated by enterprises providing
market services to the population as their main activity registered
in the same period an increase in annual terms of 14.6% (Chart
2.12), by 3.7 percentage points higher than in 2018.

Wholesale trade
In 2019,
the volume of turnover generated by wholesale
enterprises as their main activity increased by 14.7% (current
prices) compared to 2018, increasing by 7.3 percentage points
compared to 2018 (Chart 2.13). At the same time, the turnover
generated by enterprises providing market services to enterprises
as their main activity registered in the same period an increase in
annual terms of 8.2% (current prices), by 4.1 percentage points
higher than in 2018.
Foreign trade
Data on the foreign trade of the Republic of Moldova in 2019 show
signs of signiﬁcant moderation of the annual rate of exports and
imports compared to 20187. The annual rate of exports in 2019
had a level of 2.7%, with 8.9 percentage points lower than in 2018.
The downward trend of the annual rate was determined by the
evolution of exports to the EU (Chart 2.14), which contracted in
annual terms by 1.7%. At the same time, the share of exports
to EU countries accounted for about 2/3 of total exports, so their
contribution to the evolution of total exports was dominant. The
main factors that determined the dynamics of exports to the
EU were the moderation of economic activity in the EU and the
depreciation trend of the European single currency against the
US dollar in 2019. At the same time, exports to CIS countries
and those classiﬁed as the rest of the world increased by 4.6 and
18.5%, thus contributing to the increase in total exports by 0.7
and 3.2 percentage points, respectively.
The moderation of the annual export rate in 2019 was determined
by the evolution of the export of "vehicles, optical instruments,
sound recorders and reproducers", "textiles and clothing" and
"metal, stone or ceramic articles" (Chart 2.15). In 2019 there was
a contraction of re-exports, a dynamic associated mainly with the
volume of production in free economic zones, but also with the
demand from the EU. At the same time, the exports of domestic
goods generated a positive contribution, but lower than in the
previous year (Chart 2.16).
The annual import rate recorded in 2019 a level of 1.4%, by 17.9
percentage points lower than in 2018. The signiﬁcant moderation

from all categories of countries (Chart 2.17). In 2019, the share

for 24.0 and 26.7%, respectively.

market indicators reﬂect a signiﬁcant decrease in the number of
the employed population and the economically active population
during 2019, as well as an increase in the unemployment rate.
Among the main changes in the research methodology "Labour
Force Survey" there can be mentioned the use of the number
of population with habitual residence, recalculated based on the
results of the census of population and housing in 2014. Another
important change is that the employed population includes only
people engaged in the production of goods or provision of services
for payment or proﬁt9. The methodological changes mentioned
above have generated a pronounced negative dynamic for the
economically active and employed population. In the structure,
the contracting was mainly determined by the decrease of the
population employed in agriculture, an effect associated with the
exclusion of people employed in the auxiliary household with
the production of agricultural products for the household’s own
consumption.
The recalculation of labour market indicators for 2017 and 2018,
in accordance with the provisions of the new methodology,
allowed the interpretation of the evolution of 2019 compared to
previous years. Thus, after the favorable evolution in 2018, during
2019 both the economically active and the employed population
registered a pronounced negative dynamics, decreasing by 9.7%
and 11.7%, respectively, compared to 2018 (Chart 2.19). At the
same time, the activity rate was 42.3%, by 3.6 percentage points
lower than in 2018, and the employment rate was 40.1%, with 4.4
percentage points lower than in 2018 (Chart 2.26).
At the same time, the number of unemployed in 2019 increased by
about 17.3 thousand people, the unemployment rate being 5.1%,
being higher (by 2.1 percentage points) than in 2018 (Chart 2.27).

compared to 2018. Deﬂated by CPI, the salary fund increased by

the national economy increased by 3.0% (Chart 2.28).

In 2019, the average wage in the national economy increased by

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova

2.2
Evolution of inﬂation
Consumer Price Index (CPI)
In 2019, the CPI annual average inﬂation rate amounted to 4.8%,
being with 1.8 percentage points higher than in 2018. During 2019,
the annual inﬂation rate had a pronounced upward trajectory. This
increased from 0.9% in December 2018 to 7.5% in December 2019,
thus intersecting, in May-September 2019, the range of 5.0% ±1.5
percentage points stipulated in the Medium-term monetary policy
strategy (Chart 2.30).

In 2019, the upward trajectory of the annual inﬂation rate was
supported, according to estimates, by a positive aggregate
demand. At the same time, at the beginning of 2019 the upward
trajectory of the annual inﬂation rate was determined by some
adjustments of the ﬁscal policy such as the increase of excise
duties and the setting of the minimum cigarette selling price,
which accelerated the growth of core inﬂation (Chart 2.31), as well
as the effect of a reduced base period in the ﬁrst quarter of 2018
for regulated prices. Subsequently, this trend was supported by
the depreciation of the national currency, as well as the incidence
of adverse weather conditions for some crops, which exerted
inﬂationary pressures on food prices and a growing contribution
by the end of the year from this subcomponent (Chart 2.32). In
the third quarter of 2019, regulated prices generated additional
pressures as a result of the adjustment of the electricity tariff. At
the same time, towards the end of 2019, the above-mentioned
inﬂationary pressures were partially mitigated by the atypical hot
weather which delayed and diminished the impact of the positive
seasonal effect characteristic of that period on food and fuel
prices.
Core inﬂation10
Similar to the annual CPI rate, in the reference year, the annual
growth rate of the core base inﬂation followed a upward but less
pronounced trajectory. This was in the range of the inﬂation target
except for December 2019, increasing from 2.3% in December
2018 to 6.9% in December 2019. The upward trend of the core
inﬂation was more signiﬁcant in the ﬁrst six months of 2019, being
supported mainly by the effects of adjusting the ﬁscal policy for
2019, as well as inﬂationary pressures exerted by the depreciation
of the exchange rate in the ﬁrst half of the year. At the same time,
according to estimates, aggregate demand exerted inﬂationary
pressures on the core inﬂation in 2019.
10Core inﬂation is calculated by the National Bureau of Statistics by excluding
prices outside the area inﬂuenced by monetary policy measures promoted by the
National Bank of Moldova, such as food and beverages, fuels, products and services
with regulated prices. The data elaborated and published as of February 2012 are
calculated by the NBS on the basis of the amendment to Annex no. 2 of the "Core
Inﬂation Index Methodology", approved by the joint order of the National Bank
of Moldova and the National Bureau of Statistics N8-07-01203/6 of February 19,
2012 (the amendment consists of the inclusion of prices for services of remote
communication and medicines under regulated prices).


24
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
As previously mentioned, at the beginning of 2019, a major impact
on the increase in the core inﬂation was of a ﬁscal nature –
the increase in excise duties on cigarettes11 and the additional
regulations on their minimum marketing price12, which caused
a sharp rise in cigarette prices and a signiﬁcant impact during
the reference period. As a result of the common impact of those
adjustments, in 2019 prices for the "cigarettes" component of
the CPI increased by 28.9%. At the same time, some inﬂationary

pressures during the year were determined by the incidence of

In the structure in 2019, in addition to the major impact of cigarette
prices, positive contributions to the core inﬂation were exercised
by the subcomponents "clothing", "footwear", "public nutrition",
"education and training" (Chart 2.33), being generated, to a certain
extent, by the increase of costs, as a result of the adjustment of
the electricity tariff from the second half of August 201913, but
also by the increase of salaries during 2019.
Food prices
During 2019, the annual rate of food prices recorded a pronounced
upward dynamic, so it increased with 9.4 percentage points, to
12.4% in December 2019. The upward dynamics of the annual
rate was mainly driven by increase of contributions generated
by the groups "fresh fruits", "meat, meat products and canned
meat", "potatoes", "milk and milk products" and "milling and
bakery products" (Chart 2.34).

The
increase
in
prices
for
fresh
fruits
and
potatoes
was
determined by a more modest harvest for these products, caused
by adverse weather conditions in the summer of 2019. It should
be noted that during 2019 inﬂationary pressures were recorded
from processed goods14. The increase in prices for these product
categories was generated by the increase of production costs
(wage costs, raw materials, electricity, depreciation in annual
terms of the MDL/USD exchange rate), but also as a result of a
positive aggregate demand.
International food prices also had an upward trajectory during
2019, so their annual rate increased with 16.7 percentage points,
to the level of 12.2% in December 2019.
Regulated prices
In the ﬁrst half of 2019, the annual rate of regulated prices had
a relatively stable dynamics, remaining in the negative level and
registering an average value of -2.4%.
Subsequently, in the
second half of the year it outlined a pronounced upward trajectory,
increasing from -2.3% in June 2019 to 2.4% in December 2019
(Chart 2.35).

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
25
The negative dynamics of regulated prices in the ﬁrst period of the
year was mainly determined by the decrease in the electricity tariff
in July 2018 by about 10%, as well as the continuous decrease
in drug prices.
On the other hand, at the beginning of 2019,
some tariff increases were registered. Thus, the prices of medical
services in the ﬁrst two months of 2019 increased cumulatively
by about 3.3%. Starting with August 2019, the annual rate of
regulated prices entered positive territory, mainly due to the
increase of the electricity tariff15. Thus, electricity prices during
that period increased by 9.8%, following the approval of the new
tariffs by ANRE on 16 August, 2019. Among the factors invoked
for the increase of tariffs were mentioned: the evolution of the
average annual exchange rate of the national currency against
the US dollar for 2019; the increase of the average import price
of electricity, the amount of the annual average consumer price
index for 2019; new investments, as well as the increase of the
guaranteed minimum amount of the salary in the real sector.
Also, starting with the second half of 2019, positive contributions
were reported on the dynamics of regulated prices from drug
prices. During the year, price ﬂuctuations were recorded for air
and rail transport and rent in student dormitories. However, their
impact on regulated price dynamics was minor.

In 2019, the annual rate of fuel prices had a downward dynamic.
Thus, it decreased from 7.2% in January 2019 to a negative level
of 1.2% by the end of the year (Chart 2.36). This dynamic was
mainly supported by the decrease in fuel prices, as a result of
the decrease in international oil prices (the average price of Urals
oil in 2019 was 63.7 US dollars/barrel, 8.7% less, compared to
the average oil price in 2018).
The impact was mitigated by
the depreciation of the national currency against the US dollar.
Thus, in 2019 the average exchange rate was 17.5735 MDL/USD,
depreciating by 4.6% compared to the previous year. It is worth
mentioning that in the ﬁrst part of 2019, the Pricing Methodology
of Petroleum Products was revised16. According to it, ANRE will no
longer approve the maximum retail prices for petroleum products,
and the determination of the cost of fuel at ﬁlling stations has
reverted to the operators, taking into account their own costs and
applying the rate of return of no more than 10%.
At the same time, the downward dynamics of the annual rate, in
the reference period, was supported by the evolution of cylinder
gas prices. Their contribution decreased from 1.0 percentage point
at the beginning of 2019 to -1.4 percentage points at the end of
2019.
During 2019, the contribution from prices for ﬁrewood gradually
decreased to the level of 1 percentage points towards the end of
2019. At the same time, a positive but modest contribution (0.1
percentage points) to the formation of the annual fuel rate was
determined by coal prices.

The signiﬁcant increase in the annual
rate of prices in the processing industry was generated by the
increase in prices in the food industry, due to the increase in
prices for products in the group "processing and preserving of
fruits and vegetables", "processing and preserving of meat and
meat products", "manufacture of other foodstuffs (sugar)" and
"manufacture of milk products".
At the same time, prices in

upward trend, but the impact on prices was more modest.
Prices in construction
During 2019, the annual rate of construction prices had a
downward trajectory. In the fourth quarter of 2019, it stood at
3.6%, being 3.4 percentage points lower than in the ﬁrst quarter
of 2019 (Chart 2.39).
Producer’s prices for agricultural products
In 2019, producer prices on agricultural products increased by 3.8%
compared to the previous year. This evolution was determined by
the increase of prices for vegetable products by 2.4% and those
for animal products by 10.6% (Chart 2.40).
The evolution of prices of vegetal products was mainly determined
by the increase in prices of "potatoes" (31.6%), "fruit and berries"
(17.1%), "sugar beet" (14.4%), "vegetables" (12.7%) and "rape"
(11.7%).
At the same time, the upward trend in prices for animal products
was determined by the increase in prices for meat (live mass) by
13.1% and milk by 3.6%. At the same time, the price of eggs
decreased by 4.8%.

2.3
Public sector and tax policy
Implementing the national public budget
During 2019, the national public budget indicators had a more
modest evolution compared to the previous year, and the budget
deﬁcit fell within the allowable limits. As a result of public revenues
increase, of the public expenditure on a larger scale, the budget
deﬁcit enhanced and amounted to MDL 3,022.7 milion. According
to the Ministry of Finance, the revenues of the national public
budget accounted for MDL 62,949.2 milion, cu 8.6% above the
level of revenues accrued in 2018 (Chart 2.41). Compared to the

GDP, the collected revenues collected in 2019 amounted to 30.0%,
up to 0.1 percentage points less than the previous year.
The major part of the budget revenues was gathered from taxes
and fees, which amounted to 63.6% (MDL 40,054.2 milion). The
contributions and compulsory insurance premiums amounted to
29.2% (MDL 18,403.9 milion), the share of other income and grants
amounted to – 4.6% (MDL 2,888.5 milion) and respectively, 2.5%
(MDL 1,602.6 milion) of the total budget revenues.
During the reporting year, the Republic of Moldova received
ﬁnancial support to support the budget in the form of external
grants in the amount of MDL 1,602.6 million or 339.6% more than
in 2018. Reported to GDP, the grants accounted for 0.8%, by 0.6
percentage points more than in 2018. The assistance provided by
international ﬁnancial organizations and donor countries is an
important contribution to economic recovery and implementation
of reforms necessary for the modernization and relaunch of the
Republic of Moldova.
The expenditures of the national public budget amounted up to
MDL 65,971.9 milion, during the reference year, with an increase
of 10.7% compared to 2018.
Of the total expenditures, the
social protection expenditures had the highest share (35.4%), the
education expenditures amounted for 18.4%, and the healthcare
13.1%.
Public expenditures, as per the GDP, amounted up to
31.4% or by 0.5 percentage points more than in 2018.
It should be mentioned that, during 2019, the amount of MDL
23,098.8 million was transferred from the state budget (SB) to
the state social insurance budget (SSIB), the Mandatory Health
Insurance Fund (MHIF) and the local budgets (LB) the amount of
MDL 23,098.8 million (increasing by 16.9% compared to 2018).
The majority of transfers from the state budget were directed to
local budgets, about 53.4% of total transfers, followed by transfers
to SSIB, about 34.4%, the MHIF beneﬁting from a more modest
funding of 12.2%.
In this context, in 2019, the implementation of the national public
budget resulted in a deﬁcit of MDL 3,022.7 million. Relative to GDP,
this deﬁcit represents 1.4%, with 0.6 percentage points above the
level of 2018. The sources of ﬁnancing the national public deﬁcit
consisted of net external loans amounting to MDL 422.9 million,
which represents 0.2% of the GDP (0.1 percentage points less


28
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
than in 2018), from domestic debts and loans in the amount of
MDL 140.9 million or 0.1% of GDP (0.1 percentage points decrease
compared to the previous year) and ﬁnancial assets in amounting
to MDL 521.9 million, which accounted for 0.2% of GDP (similar
to the level recorded in 2018). At the same time, the balances of
funds in the accounts of the component budgets of the national
public budget at the end of the year amounted to MDL 4,673.6
million.

Balance of payments17
According to preliminary data, in 2019, the balance of the current
account of the balance of payments of the Republic of Moldova
amounted a deﬁcit of USD 1,159.30 million, which decreased by
4.3% compared to the one recorded in 2018. Reported to GDP, the
current account amounted to -9.7% (compared to -10.6% in 2018)
(Chart 2.42, table A.1). The source of the current account deﬁcit
was foreign trade in goods, with the other components registering
surplus balances.

The deﬁcit in trade with goods amounted to USD 3,312.77 million18,
deepening by 0.6%, as imports increased more (+159.98 million
USD) than exports (+141.21 million USD), compared to 2018. The
ratio between the deﬁcit balance of foreign trade in goods and
GDP was 27.7%, (compared to -28.8% in 2018).
The export of goods registered in the balance of payments
(excluding goods after/for processing) amounted up to USD
2,116.26 million (+7.1% compared to 2018).
Despite the
unfavorable evolution of prices for exported goods, the value of
exports increased due to the increase in physical volumes.
The geographical structure of exports of goods in 2019 reﬂects
the fact that 57.9% were directed to EU countries, 21.3% were
directed to CIS countries, and the remaining 20.8% of exports were
intended for other countries (Chart 2.43). The largest contribution
to the increase in exports was made by other countries, exports
to them increasing by 26.6% compared to 2018. Exports to EU
countries increased by 1.8%, and to CIS countries – by 4.6%.
In exports of goods, as usual, agri-food products predominated
(increasing by 4.5% compared to 2018), with a share of 56.7%
of the total. Exports of machinery and equipment (mainly wires
and cables), with a share of 15.3% of total exports, continued the
upward trend, increasing by 32.4% compared to 2018. Export of
articles and parts furniture decreased by 5.4%, chemical products
increased by 19.7%.
Exports of textile materials and articles
decreased by 21.1%.
The import of goods registered in the balance of payments
(excluding goods after/for processing) amounted to USD 5,429.03
million (+3.0% compared to 2018). The increase in the import of
goods, fueled by the increase in gross ﬁxed capital formation and
the increase in ﬁnal consumption of households, slowed during
the year, to stagnation in the fourth quarter of 2019.
This is
explained by the fact that although household incomes increased

In the geographical structure of imports of goods (according to the
country of delivery principle), in 2019, the majority share went
to the European Union – 54.9% (Chart 2.44), the value of imports
from the EU increasing by 3.3%, compared to 2018. Imports from
the CIS registered a negative dynamics (-1.4%) and had a share of
28.2%. Imports from other countries increased by 7.5% compared
to 2018, accounting for 16.9% of total imports.
The main categories of goods imported in 2019 were: mineral
products, with a share of 17.4% (lower by 6.7%), machinery and
appliances (+6.6%), agri-food products (+5.9%), chemical industry
products (+5.8%), common metals and articles thereof (-0.3%),
means and equipment of transport (+5.2%).
Imports of energy products and electricity in 2019 amounted to
USD 894.26 million, a decrease of 6.3% compared to 2018. This
development was determined by the decrease in imports of diesel
fuels by 4.8%, electricity by 27.6% and gasoline by 7.6% (Chart
2.45).
The decrease in the positive balance of services by 2.4% to USD
352.83 million was caused by the increase in imports (+6.0%) and
the slowdown in the increase in exports (3.9%) compared to 2018.
The ratio between the balance of services and GDP was 3.0%, with
0.2 percentage points lower than in 2018 (Chart 2.46). The decline
in the balance of services was largely due to the decline in exports
of transport services (-5.9%), especially air freight (according to
a statement issued by IATA19, 2019 was globally the worst in the
last ten years for air freight).
We also note the continuous decrease (since 2013) and sharp
decline in exports of telecommunications services (-27.2%
compared to 2018). The negative impact was mitigated by the
signiﬁcant increase in exports of IT services (+32.1%) and other
business services (+19.6%).
The export of raw material
processing services increased during 2019 by 6.8%, but it should
be noted that in the second half of the year it had a negative
dynamic. The increase in imports of services resulted mainly from
the increase in imports of travel services (+9.6%) and transport
(+4.0%).
The surplus of primary incomes increased in 2019 by 5.1%
compared to 2018, to USD 576.31 million, amounting to 4.8% of
GDP (Chart 2.47). Remuneration of resident employees for work
performed by non-resident employers (credit) increased by 3.6%
to USD 975.88 million. At the same time, there was a substantial
increase in payments in the form of dividends paid to foreign
direct investors (+29.4% compared to 2018), which inﬂuenced the
decrease in primary income in the sense of decrease.
The secondary income balance amounted to USD 1,224.33 million
(10.2% of GDP), increasing by 4.4% compared to 2018 (Chart 2.48).

increase of personal transfers from residents to non-residents
(debit) continued, which in 2019 amounted to USD 99.03 million
(+17.5% compared to 2018).
Personal
remittances20
received by the residents of the
Republic of Moldova in 2019 amounted to USD 1,812.44 million
(+4.0% compared to 2018), which represents 15.2% relative to
GDP (unchanged compared to 2018). The increase in personal
remittances was mainly determined by the increase in ﬂows from
EU countries (+17.3% compared to 2018), but also by the
increase in remittances from other countries (+1.1%).
At the
same time, CIS ﬂows decreased by 10.6% compared to 2018
(Chart 2.49).
The capital account had a negative balance of USD 52.08 million,
which deteriorated by 44.9% compared to 2018.
The capital
account balance was determined by the net capital outﬂows
recorded by the private sector (USD 73.01 million), while the
inﬂows of external assistance received by the public administration
to ﬁnance investment projects amounted to USD 20.95 million.
The sum of the current account and capital account balances
shows that, as a result of current and capital transactions of
Moldovan residents with non-residents,
the net
need
for
external ﬁnancing amounted to USD 1,211.38 million (10.1% of
GDP).
The ﬁnancial account of the balance of payments recorded a
considerable increase in net capital inﬂows in the form of foreign
direct investment (especially in the second quarter of 2019).
Another evolution was the resumption of external lending starting
with the second quarter of 2019, after its partial suspension at the
beginning of the year in the context of the uncertainty related to
the parliamentary elections.
However, the main source of
ﬁnancing the current account deﬁcit in 2019 was the assets in the
form of cash and deposits of the population.
Thus, the ﬁnancial account during 2019 resulted in net capital
inﬂows amounting to USD 1,205.94 million as a result of the net
increase of residents’ liabilities to non-residents by USD 694.37
million generated by actual transactions, simultaneously with the
net decrease of residents’ foreign funding assets by USD 511.57
million (Chart 2.50).
Liabilities increased, in particular, due to the net increase in
commitments in the form of direct investments of USD 593.01
million. The value of equity participations (other than reinvestment
of proﬁts) in the possession of non-residents increased, in 2019,
as a result of actual transactions, by USD 426.93 million; the
reinvestment of proﬁts was valued at USD 76.00 million, and the

same period amounted to USD 125.50 million. The National Bank
of Moldova received 18.48 million from the IMF. USD, but repaid
USD 55.38 million of previously contracted loans. The licensed
banks made net repayments in the amount of USD 21.54 million,
and the other sectors – net withdrawals of USD 93.58 million.
Liabilities also increased by USD 50.66 million during 2019, as a
result of residents receiving net trade credits and advances from
non-resident trading partners.
At the same time, cash and deposit liabilities decreased by a
net decrease of USD 4.86 million, due to withdrawals from non-
residents’ accounts opened in resident commercial banks.
The decrease in ﬁnancial assets was due to the net decrease in
assets in the form of cash and deposits by USD 537.56 million,
of which the assets of licensed banks increased by USD 46.33
million and the assets of other sectors decreased by USD 583.89
million). Non-resident trading partners made net payments of USD
97.51 million on trade loans and advances previously granted by
Moldovan residents, thereby reducing foreign ﬁnancial assets.
At the same time, the net increase of the ofﬁcial reserve assets
of the NBM by USD 60.49 million during 2019 inﬂuenced the total
assets in the sense of growth. Also, residents’ external assets
increased by USD 46.94 million in the form of direct investments,
consisting mainly of participations in the capital of foreign-afﬁliated
enterprises. Net foreign assets also increased by USD 11.52 million
in residents’ foreign assets in the form of loans to non-residents.
Residents’ external assets in the form of portfolio investments
increased by USD 4.55 million in net worth, determined by the
investments of licensed banks in debt securities.

on 31 December 2019, amounted to – USD 5,084.65 million (-

The evolution of the balance of the international investment
position, in 2019, was negatively determined by the actual net
transactions with non-residents (-1,205.94 million USD) and by
the price changes of ﬁnancial instruments (-81.61 million USD),
while the ﬂuctuation exchange rates of the original currencies
against the US dollar (38.81 million USD) and other changes
(362.19 million USD) positively inﬂuenced the position balance. All
the mentioned changes resulted in a total deterioration of the
investment position on 31.12.2019 by USD 886.55 million.
Within foreign funding assets, the largest share (57.6%) belonged
to reserve assets. During 2019, the stock of ofﬁcial reserve assets
increased by 2.2% compared to the stock at the beginning of the
year, amounting to USD 3,059.63 million as of 31 December 2019.
The stock of ofﬁcial reserve assets increased due to the net balance
of payments in the balance of payments by USD 60.49 million
(calculated at the daily exchange rate), the ﬂuctuation of the
exchange rates of the original currencies against the US dollar
by USD 2.03 million and changes in the price of securities and
monetary gold by USD 1.93 million.
The volume of ofﬁcial reserve assets corresponds to all sufﬁciency
criteria: coverage of at least 3 months of imports of goods and
services (covered 5.6 months of actual imports); full coverage
of short-term external debt by reserves (they covered 141.7%);
covering 20% of the M2 money supply (they covered 83.4%);
coverage of 100-150% of the amount: 30% of short-term debt
+15% of other commitments +5% of M2 +5% of exports of goods
and services (covered 196.9%) (Chart 2.53).
In the structure of external liabilities, other investments with a
weight of 53.2% predominated, followed by direct investments
with a weight of 46.5%.
The total stock of liabilities in the form of direct investments
accumulated on 31.12.2019 was valued at USD 4,836.36 million
(increasing by 16.2% compared to 31 December 2018).
The share of FDI in the form of participations and shares
constituted 58.4% of the total stock, the rest belonging to debt
instruments (Chart 2.54). Under debt instruments, the largest
share – 41.6% – is the stock of loans taken from foreign direct
investors, the rest are commitments in the form of trade credits
and other debts to non-resident investors.
In the geographical distribution of direct investments in the form
of participations and accumulated shares, investors from EU
countries accounted for the majority share – 83.8%.

The main economic activities that attracted foreign investment are:

industry (21.7% of the direct investment stock in the form of

2.5
Public
Debt
and
External
Public
Debt of the Republic of Moldova
Public debt
At the end of 2019, the public debt of the Republic of Moldova
amounted to MDL 52,494.3 million, representing 25.0% of the GDP.
The public debt increased, during 2019, by MDL 481.8 million or
by 0.9%, due to the increase of the balance of the external public
debt by MDL 372.2 million and the balance of the internal public
debt by MDL 109.6 million. As a share in GDP, both internal and
external public debt decreased in 2019 by 1.0 percentage point
(Chart 2.56).

As of 31 December 2019, the public debt was formed by the
external public debt in proportion of 55.9% and the internal public
debt in proportion of 44.1%, registering insigniﬁcant changes
compared to the end of 2018.
The external public debt balance, in foreign currency, as of 31
December 2019, increased by 0.9% compared to 31 December
2018 and amounted to USD 1,704.1 million. The external public
debt, in MDL, totaled at the end of the reporting year MDL 29,326.1
million, a level higher than at the end of 2018 by 1.3%.
The internal public debt, at the end of 2019, registered MDL
23,168.2 million, higher than the one at the end of 2018 by 0.5%.
The increase in internal public debt was due to additional SS issues
on the primary market by about 4.3%. It should be mentioned that
in 2019 the SS issued on 4 October 2016 were redeemed for the
execution of payment obligations derived from state guarantees,
in the amount of MDL 230.0 million. Therefore, at the end of 2019,
the internal debt consisted of SS issued for the execution of state
guarantees (55.5%), SS issued on the primary market (35.6%) and
converted SS (8.9%).
In 2019, for the service of the public debt from the national public
budget, there were used funds in the amount of MDL 1,619.5
million (by 7.6% more compared to 2018). The service of external
public debt constituted MDL 412.1 million (by 8.6% more compared
to 2018), and the service of internal public debt constituted MDL
1,207.4 million (by 7.3% less compared to 2018).

improved compared to the situation on 31 December 2018 by 5.1
percentage points.

(consisting of advances received for exports and unpaid invoices
for imports) – 85.5%, bank deposits (term and on sight) of non-
residents in licensed banks in the Republic of Moldova – 6.2%,
other debt commitments – 4.2%, intragroup loans – 0.7%, other
short-term loans – 3.4% (Chart 2.59).
From the point of view of the foreign currency structure of external
loans, those under public and publicly guaranteed debt were
mainly in SDRs (with a share of 54.0%), while external loans under
private nonguaranteed debt were contracted, mainly in US dollars
– 49.7% and in euros with a share of 46.5%.


Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
37
Relations of the National Bank with the Government of the
Republic of Moldova
Government debt to the National Bank
As of 31 December 2019, the public debt to the National Bank
in the form of state securities in the NBM’s portfolio totaled MDL
14,914.6 million and consisted of:
• Government bonds (GB) issued and transmitted to the
National Bank on 4 October 2016 in order to execute the
obligations of the Ministry of Finance derived from state
guarantees no. 807 of November 17, 2014 and no. 101 of
April 1, 2015 – MDL 12,851.2 million. They have an interest
rate (coupon) of 1.40% for maturities of 4-9 years and 5.30%
for maturities of 10-25 years;
• State securities obtained in the NBM portfolio following the
conversion of loans previously contracted by the Ministry of
Finance from the NBM – MDL 2,063.4 million. During 2019,
SS obtained in the NBM’s own portfolio as a result of reissues
had a maturity of 1 year (BT) with an interest rate of 6.59%
and 2 years (SO) with an interest rate (coupon) of 6.20% and
6.30%.
State deposits placed at the National Bank of Moldova
As of 31 December 2019, the balance of term deposits of the
Ministry of Finance placed with the National Bank amounted to MDL
253.5 million, consisting exclusively of deposits for the formation
of the liquidity reserve. These were set up in 2018, for a period
of 2 to 5 years, in order to form the liquidity reserve needed to
reduce the risk of reﬁnancing the public debt.
The weighted average rate on balance deposits registered 6.23%
annually, and the weighted average term – 1111 days.
During 2019, the Ministry of Finance placed deposits with the
National Bank only for the purpose of managing the resources of
the treasury single account (MDL 28.0 million), maturing until 31
December 2019.


38
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
Chapter 3
Monetary Policy
3.1
Monetary policy objective
In accordance with the provisions of Law on the National Bank of
Moldova no.548-XIII of 21 July 1995, the NBM is the central bank of
the Republic of Moldova, being an autonomous public legal entity
that establishes and promotes monetary and foreign exchange
policy in the state. During 2018, the NBM operated in accordance
with the provisions of the Medium-term monetary policy strategy,
approved by the Council of Administration of the National Bank of
Moldova on 27 December 2012.
According to the strategy, in order to ensure and maintain price
stability, the NBM set the continuous inﬂation target, measured
by the consumer price index, published monthly by the National
Bureau of Statistics (NBS), at the level of 5.0% with a possible
deviation of ±1.5 percentage points.
During 2019, the National Bank of Moldova promoted a monetary
policy, aiming to ensure real monetary conditions adequate to
support lending and saving, along with adapting the domestic
economic environment to external volatilities and uncertainties.
At the same time, the conduct of monetary policy was affected by
the complexity of the balance of both internal and external risks,
with the gradual intensiﬁcation of inﬂationary risks.
3.2
Monetary policy decisions
The conduct of monetary policy continued to take shape in line
with the risks and uncertainties associated with developments
in the external and internal environment. The activation of the
inﬂationary process at the beginning of 2019 was conﬁrmed by
the forecasts of the National Bank, according to which the annual
inﬂation rate had an ascending trajectory over the limits of the
variation corridor of ±1.5 percentage points from the inﬂation
target until the fourth quarter of 2019.
Thus, during 2019, the National Bank of Moldova undertook
measures to gradually calibrate monetary policy, given the
aggregate demand that generates inﬂationary pressures, the
increase of the salary fund, pensions, consumer loans, as well as
the positive ﬁscal impulse. In this context, the Executive Board of
the NBM decided to increase by 0.5 percentage points the
monetary policy interest rate in June and July 2019 to the level of
7.5% annually.


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
39
At the last monetary policy meeting in 2019, the Executive Board of
the NBM decided to reduce the base rate by 2.0 percentage points,
to 5.5% annually. This easing monetary policy adjustment decision
was based on inﬂation forecasts, the dynamics of which showed
a decline during 2020 under the impact of declining aggregate
demand due to weakening consumer and external demand due to
the slowdown in global economic activity, the relative stagnation
of international food and commodity price growth rates (Chart
3.1).
Chart 3.1: The CPI forecast published in Inﬂation
Report no.4, 2019 (%)
Source: NBM
It should be mentioned that, within the meeting of 11 December
2019, the National Bank of Moldova reduced the norm of the
minimum required reserves from the funds attracted in Moldovan
lei and in non-convertible currency by 1.5 percentage points up to
the value of 41.0%, the application period being in the ﬁrst quarter
of 2020. At the same time, the norm of required reserves from
the means attracted in freely convertible currency was increased
in July 2019 by 3.0 percentage points, up to the level of 17.0%
and in December 2019 by 3.0 percentage points, up to the level of
20.0%, the last decision being applied in the ﬁrst quarter of 2020.
This mix of monetary policy measures aimed to anchor inﬂation
expectations in terms of consolidating the prospects of maintaining
the inﬂation rate within the range of ±1.5 percentage points
from the target of 5.0%. This objective was to be achieved by
encouraging the wider use of the national currency in the process
of saving and investing, as well as in lending.
That decision
was intended to help boost demand for private and government
consumption, boost investment and support foreign trade.
At
the same time, the decision on decreasing the norm of required
reserves from funds attracted in Moldovan lei and non-convertible
currency and increasing the norm of required reserves from funds
attracted in freely convertible currency aimed at encouraging
ﬁnancial intermediation in national currency and, respectively,
mitigating that in foreign currency.
3.3
Monetary policy achievements
Money market operations, standing facilities and the reserve
mechanism continued to play a key role in the implementation
of monetary policy.
The central bank has adapted its set of
monetary policy instruments to the new context created by the
trend of gradual narrowing of the liquidity surplus in the banking
sector, resuming, starting with 30 July 2019, the repo operations
of liquidity payment. This change came after in the ﬁrst half of the
year the NBM used exclusively the NBM certiﬁcates for draining
the excess liquidity. The permanent facilities were used at the
discretion of the banks, both of which were requested during the
year. The norm of required reserves related to liabilities in MDL
and in non-convertible currencies of banks was maintained at the
level of the previous year. At the same time, in order to encourage
ﬁnancial intermediation in MDL, the norm of required reserves from
the means attracted in freely convertible currencies was increased
by 3.0 percentage points.


40
Annual Report (NBM, 2019)
Monetary market operations
Issuance of NBM certiﬁcates
The gradual narrowing of the liquidity surplus was reﬂected in
the evolution of the NBM’s monetary policy operations. Thus, the
volume of operations intended for liquidity absorption decreased
by about 34% compared to 2018, a situation also reﬂected in the
annual average of the stock of investments NBC, which decreased
from MDL 8,356.8 million, a level recorded in 2018, to MDL 5,500.1
million in 2019 (Chart 3.2).
The daily balance of CBN registered both the maximum value of
MDL 7,368.3 million and the minimum of MDL 4,124.7 million in
the second half of March, following the NBM’s interventions as a
seller on the foreign exchange market during January – February.
The balance of CBN investments at the end of 2019 amounted to

MDL 5,396.1 million.
The NBM conducted weekly CBN placement auctions, with a
maturity of 14 days and announcing the maximum interest rate
equivalent to the base rate of the NBM. For the whole of 2019, the
weighted average rate of CBN placed amounted to 6.86%,
compared to 6.50% recorded in the previous year.
Repo operations
The National Bank resumed, starting with 30 July 2019, the repo
operations of liquidity payment, offering additional support to
the banks in liquidity management. These were carried out at
the initiative of the National Bank on a weekly basis through the
procedure of auctions without a ceiling, according to the updated
calendar of monetary policy operations. Thus, the NBM conducted
14-day repo tenders at a ﬁxed rate (base rate plus a margin of
0.25 percentage points); the volume of liquidity delivered was MDL
350.0 million.
Permanent facilities
The operation regime of the permanent facilities (deposits and
overnight loans) established by the National Bank in 2019 offered
the banks an extra ﬂexibility in the efﬁcient management of their
own liquidities, in the conditions of diminishing the excess liquidity
during the year.
Interest rates on permanent facilities during 2019, unlike the
previous year when they were maintained at the same level, were
increased in two successive stages, by 0.50 percentage points, in
June and July, after which they were decreased in December with
2.0 percentage points. Thus, the rate on overnight deposits at
the end of the year was 2.50%, and the rate on overnight loans –
8.50%.
The amplitude of the symmetrical corridor formed by the two
permanent facilities around the base rate was maintained at ±3.0
percentage points.
Overnight deposit facility
The year-round use of the deposit facility by banks to absorb the
net surplus from the banking system has meant a better ability of
banks to manage funds in the short term.


Chapter 3. Monetary Policy
41
Banks have resorted to the overnight deposit facility preferably
towards the end of the required reserve periods. The frequency
of applications for the deposit facility followed, broadly, a similar
pattern during all periods of application of required reserves in
Moldovan lei: the amounts placed overnight by banks at the NBM
were at relatively lower levels at the beginning of the period,
increasing later, as banks fulﬁlled their obligation to maintain
required reserves.
The total volume of overnight deposits placed by banks with the
National Bank in 2019 amounted to MDL 75.94 billion, which

The average daily balance of banks’ overnight investments
calculated for each month was uneven. Thus, in the ﬁrst months
of 2019 the balance registered higher values, this segment having
the annual maximum registered in March (MDL 628.1 million);
after which, simultaneously with the decrease of the liquidity
surplus in the banking system, the balance of overnight deposits
decreases signiﬁcantly, the minimum (MDL 103.3 million) being
registered in August, in order to increase slightly towards the end
of the year.
Overnight credit facility
The decrease in excess liquidity pursued in the banking system in
2019 also inﬂuenced the banks’ requests for obtaining overnight
loans.
Thus, the total amount of overnight loans granted by the NBM in
2019 amounted to MDL 1,055 million, being over 2.5 times higher
than in the previous year (MDL 411 million).
The credit facility was requested, in particular, at the end of the
required reserve periods by a small number of banks, in order
to comply with the requirements set out in the required reserves
regime.
Required reserves
The reserve requirement mechanism continued to fulﬁll the
functions of monetary control and liquidity management in the
banking sector.
During 2019, amid the structural excess of liquidity in the banking
sector, the NBM maintained the norm of required reserves in MDL
at the level of 42.5% of the funds attracted in Moldovan lei and
in non-convertible currency. At the same time, given that during
